Command & Conquer: Generals Zero Hour. Classic RTS.

This is a serious project. Before diving in, some critical questions:

1. **Goal scope** - Beat the built-in AI? Beat humans online? Specific faction or all three (USA, China, GLA)?
2. **Replay format** - Do you have access to replay files? ZH replays (.rep files) contain command sequences, not game state. Parsing them is non-trivial.
3. **Game interface** - How will the bot interact with the game? Options:
   - Memory reading/injection (most common for RTS bots)
   - Screen capture + input simulation (harder, slower)
   - Modding the game engine (SAGE engine, partially documented)
4. **Prior art** - Are you aware of existing ZH modding/bot projects, or starting from scratch?

Key challenges I see immediately:
- ZH has no official API. Interfacing with a 2003 game engine is the hardest part.
- RTS state space is enormous compared to chess/Go. Full micro+macro is brutal.
- Replay parsing requires reverse-engineering the .rep format.

Here is a comprehensive summary of the **electronicarts/CnC_Generals_Zero_Hour** GitHub repository.

---

## Language

- **Primary language:** C++ (roughly 75.5 MB of code -- the overwhelming majority)
- **Secondary languages:** C (2.4 MB), GLSL (shader code, 48 KB), Perl, Makefile, Batchfile, HTML, CSS (all minor)

## Build System

- **Visual Studio 6.0 workspace** (`rts.dsw` / `RTS.dsp`) -- the original build system. SP6 of VC++ 6.0 is recommended for binary-matching the original Generals 1.08 and Zero Hour 1.04 patches.
- Can be **converted** to modern MSVC by opening in VS .NET 2003, then upgrading to VS 2015+, but significant C++ standard-compliance fixes are needed.
- **Win32 only.** No CMake, no cross-platform build files are provided.
- Compiled binaries are output to a `/Run/` folder in each game's directory.

## License

- **GPL v3 with additional terms.** GitHub labels it "Other" because of the extra conditions beyond standard GPL v3. The full terms are in `LICENSE.md`.

## Repo Structure

The repository is **archived** (read-only, no PRs/issues accepted) and has two parallel top-level directories plus supporting files:

```
/
  Generals/          -- C&C Generals (base game)
    Code/
      GameEngine/        -- Core engine (gameplay logic, networking, AI, etc.)
      GameEngineDevice/  -- Device/platform layer (rendering, video, input)
      Libraries/         -- Third-party libs (DirectX, STLport, ZLib, etc.)
      Main/              -- Entry point
      Tools/             -- Build/dev tools
      RTS.dsp / RTS.dsw  -- VC6 project/workspace files
    Run/                 -- Output directory for compiled binaries
  GeneralsMD/        -- C&C Generals: Zero Hour (expansion, "Mission Disc")
    Code/            -- Same sub-structure as Generals/Code
    Run/
  LICENSE.md
  README.md
  stlport.diff       -- Patch for STLport 4.5.3 to compile the source
```

The two game trees (`Generals` and `GeneralsMD`) are essentially parallel codebases with the expansion's additions layered in.

## Dependencies (notable)

DirectX 9 SDK, STLport 4.5.3, 3DS Max 4 SDK, RAD Miles Sound System, RAD Bink SDK, GameSpy SDK, ZLib 1.1.4, SafeDisk API, and others. Most of these are **proprietary/unavailable** -- the README notes you must "find or write new replacements" for them.

## API / Modding Hooks

- **Steam Workshop support** is the stated purpose of the source release. The repo explicitly provides Workshop integration for both Generals and Zero Hour on Steam.
- The engine uses an **INI-driven data system** and a **module/behavior architecture** (common in the SAGE engine family) that is inherently moddable -- game objects, weapons, upgrades, and AI are defined in data files.
- The **Xfer** serialization system (`Xfer.cpp`) provides a snapshot/transfer mechanism used across networking and save/load.
- The **GameEngine / GameEngineDevice** split is a clean abstraction boundary -- the engine logic is separated from the rendering/platform layer, which makes it feasible to port the renderer.
- No formal plugin API or SDK is provided. Modding at the source level is the intended path (fork the repo).

## Additional Context: The Open-Source Release

- **Released:** February 27, 2025 -- part of a larger EA initiative that also open-sourced C&C: Red Alert, C&C: Renegade, and the original C&C (Tiberian Dawn).
- **Stats:** approximately 4,500 stars, 1,625 forks, archived (no contributions accepted).
- **Community reception:** Overwhelmingly positive. Over 400 forks appeared within days. The community immediately began organizing a collaborative Community Patch project. The OpenSAGE project (a C# reimplementation of the SAGE engine) opened a discussion about how to leverage the official source. The **TheSuperHackers/GeneralsGameCode** fork became a central community hub for patch development. Some developers expressed interest in porting to modern engines (e.g., Unreal 5). Coverage appeared on GamingOnLinux, GIGAZINE, Worthplaying, ModDB, and GameReplays.

---

- [EA announcement: C&C Steam Workshop Support & Source Code](https://www.ea.com/games/command-and-conquer/command-and-conquer-remastered/news/steam-workshop-support)
- [GamingOnLinux: EA just open sourced Command & Conquer, Red Alert, Renegade and Generals](https://www.gamingonlinux.com/2025/02/ea-just-open-sourced-command-conquer-red-alert-renegade-and-generals/)

This is great news. EA officially open-sourced it (Feb 2025, GPL v3). C++ codebase with a clean **GameEngine / GameEngineDevice** split — that's exactly the seam we need.

Key facts:
- **GameEngine** = gameplay logic, AI, networking (what we hook into)
- **GameEngineDevice** = rendering, input (what we can skip during training)
- INI-driven data system for units/weapons/upgrades
- Win32 only, VS6 originally, but community has ported to modern MSVC

Next question:

**What's your target platform for training?** This affects the whole pipeline since the game is Win32.

- A) **Windows** — train directly on Windows, simplest path to run the game
- B) **Linux** — prefer Linux for training infra (GPU servers, cloud), would need Wine/Proton layer or port the engine
- C) **Cloud GPU** (AWS/GCP/Lambda Labs) — if so, which OS?
- D) **Don't care** — whatever works fastest

> DEVELOPER

ok we need to decide.. i'm working rn on mac m4

> AGENT

Mac M4. ARM architecture. The game is Win32/x86.

This is a critical constraint. Options:

**For training:**
- The game engine needs to run headless (no rendering) to simulate games fast
- We'd need to cross-compile or port the C++ engine to macOS/ARM — doable since we have source, but significant effort
- Alternative: rent cloud GPU boxes (Linux x86) for heavy training, develop locally on Mac

**For development:**
- Model code, replay parser, game interface layer — all can be developed on Mac
- PyTorch runs natively on M4 (MPS backend)

**My recommendation:** Develop everything on Mac. For training that requires the game engine running, use Linux x86 cloud instances (cheaper GPU access anyway). The headless engine build would target Linux.

Does that split work for you, or do you want everything runnable locally on the Mac?

> DEVELOPER

is it possible to run locally? i mean to spin up VM or smth like that?

> AGENT

Possible, but bad idea for training.

**VM/emulation options on M4 Mac:**
- **UTM/QEMU** — can emulate x86 Windows/Linux, but **software emulation is ~10-20x slower** since M4 is ARM
- **CrossOver/Wine** — translation layer, better perf than full VM, but flaky with complex C++ game engines
- **Rosetta on Linux VM** — Parallels/UTM can run x86 Linux binaries via Rosetta, ~2-3x overhead

**The problem:** Training needs to run millions of game ticks as fast as possible. Any emulation overhead kills you. A game that runs 1000 ticks/sec natively might do 50-100 in emulation. Training that takes 1 week becomes 3-6 months.

**However** — if we strip rendering and compile the game engine as a headless simulator targeting native ARM Linux/macOS, no emulation needed. We have the source. This is the right path:

1. Port the game engine core (GameEngine, not GameEngineDevice) to compile on macOS ARM
2. Replace DirectX/Win32 dependencies with stubs or cross-platform equivalents
3. Run pure game logic natively at full speed

That's real engineering work (maybe 2-4 weeks), but it gives you a native headless simulator on M4 with zero overhead.

**So the real question is:** Do you want to invest time porting the engine to run natively on Mac, or rent a cheap cloud box and skip that entirely?

> DEVELOPER

> AGENT

That changes things. Intel Mac can run the Win32 engine much more easily:

- **Boot Camp** — native Windows, full speed, zero overhead
- **Parallels/VMware** — near-native x86 performance, no emulation tax

16GB RAM is tight but workable for headless game simulation.

**Practical split:**

| Machine | Role |
|---------|------|
| **Mac Intel** | Run headless game engine (native x86), generate training data from replays, self-play |
| **Mac M4** | ML development, model training (MPS GPU), inference, main dev machine |

For heavy training later (when you need serious GPU hours), cloud is still an option. But this gets you started with zero cost.

**Next question:**

What language do you want the AI/ML pipeline in?

- A) **Python** (PyTorch) — standard choice, best ecosystem, most examples
- B) **Python + C++ bindings** — Python for ML, C++ for game interface (pybind11)
- C) Something else?

B is what I'd recommend. The game engine is C++, so the interface layer stays C++, exposed to Python via bindings. Model training in PyTorch.

> DEVELOPER

Python TF is possible? and do i need c++ or python can handle all it?

> AGENT

**TensorFlow vs PyTorch:**

TF works but I'd push back. PyTorch is the better choice here because:
- RL ecosystem is PyTorch-first (Stable Baselines3, RLlib, CleanRL)
- AlphaStar-style architectures — most reference implementations are PyTorch
- Debugging RL is hard enough without TF's graph mode quirks
- TF is in maintenance mode — Google shifted to JAX internally

If you have a strong reason for TF, it's not a blocker. But you'd be swimming upstream.

**C++ question:**

You need *some* C++ — unavoidable. The game engine is C++. You need a thin C++ layer that:
1. Reads game state from engine memory structures
2. Sends actions (build unit, move, attack) to the engine
3. Exposes this as a Python API via pybind11

Think of it like a gym environment wrapper. The C++ part is ~500-1000 lines, not a whole project. Everything else (model, training loop, replay parsing, evaluation) is pure Python.

```
Python (PyTorch + RL)  ←→  pybind11  ←→  C++ interface  ←→  Game Engine
```

**So: Python for 90% of the work, thin C++ glue layer for the game interface.**

Are you comfortable with that, or is C++ a concern?

## Three Approaches

### Approach A: AlphaStar-style (Recommended)

Closest to what DeepMind did for StarCraft II.

**Pipeline:**
1. **Imitation Learning** — train on human replays to bootstrap a policy that knows basic build orders, micro, macro
2. **Reinforcement Learning** — fine-tune via self-play against built-in AI, then self-play vs itself
3. **Architecture** — Transformer encoder for game state + spatial CNN for map + LSTM for temporal context → action decoder

**Pros:** Proven at scale for RTS. Replay bootstrapping dramatically cuts RL training time.
**Cons:** Most complex. Needs careful state/action space design. Heavy compute for self-play phase.

---

### Approach B: Hierarchical RL (Macro + Micro split)

Two separate models working together.

**Pipeline:**
1. **Macro agent** — decides strategy (build order, tech path, attack timing) every ~5-10 seconds
2. **Micro agent** — executes tactics (unit control, ability usage) at high frequency
3. Train macro from replays, micro via RL in isolated combat scenarios

**Pros:** Easier to debug (which layer is failing?). Micro can be trained on small scenarios fast.
**Cons:** Coordination between layers is hard. Two models to maintain. Handoff logic is brittle.

---

### Approach C: Pure Imitation Learning + Search

Skip RL. Learn from replays only, then use Monte Carlo Tree Search at inference time.

**Pipeline:**
1. Train a strong policy network from replays
2. At game time, use MCTS to look ahead and pick best action sequences
3. Value network trained on replay outcomes guides the search

**Pros:** Simpler training. No reward shaping headaches.
**Cons:** Ceiling is limited to human replay quality. MCTS is expensive in real-time RTS. Won't beat top players without RL.

---

### My Recommendation: **Approach A**

For your goal of beating good-to-top players, you need RL self-play — that's where superhuman play comes from. Approach A is the proven path. We use replays to get 80% of the way, RL to push beyond human level.

Approach B could be a fallback if A proves too hard to train end-to-end.

Which approach do you want to go with?

> DEVELOPER

go for A.....do i understand it right that model will adjust plan and strategy during the game based on situation?

> AGENT

Yes. The model observes game state every tick and outputs actions. There's no fixed plan — it's reactive.

But it's more nuanced than "just react":

**What the model learns implicitly from training:**
- **Long-term strategy** — build orders, tech timing, expansion patterns (learned from replays)
- **Adaptation** — if it scouts enemy going air, it shifts to anti-air (learned from thousands of replays where humans did this)
- **Tempo** — when to attack, when to defend, when to eco (emerges from RL self-play)

**The LSTM/memory component is key here.** It gives the model a sense of game history — what it scouted, what attacks happened, what the opponent's tendencies are. Without it, the model would be purely reactive (see tanks → build anti-tank). With it, the model can form and adjust plans across minutes of gameplay.

**Think of it like this:**
- Each forward pass = one decision (~100-200ms of game time)
- The model sees: current map state + unit positions + resources + fog of war + its own memory
- It outputs: what to build, where to move units, what to attack, what abilities to use
- The memory carries forward context like "I saw enemy building air units 30 seconds ago"

So yes — it's constantly adjusting strategy based on the evolving situation. Not a scripted plan.

Ready to move to the detailed design?

> DEVELOPER

yep..but we need to have high level plan or set of plans, in case of different opponent strategies from the start.

> AGENT

I'd push back slightly on *hardcoded* plans. Here's why:

**Hardcoded plans are brittle.** If you define "anti-rush plan" and "anti-air plan," the model becomes a glorified decision tree. Real games have blended strategies — opponent rushes with light vehicles while teching to air simultaneously.

**Better approach: learned strategy conditioning.**

The model has two heads:

1. **Strategy encoder** — classifies the current game situation into a strategy embedding (not a fixed label, a continuous vector). Trained on replays where we label phases: early aggression, fast expand, tech rush, turtle, etc.

2. **Policy head** — takes strategy embedding + game state → actions

This way:
- The model *discovers* strategic archetypes from data, not from our definitions
- It can blend strategies ("70% defensive, 30% preparing counter-attack")
- It adapts fluidly as opponent strategy shifts mid-game

**What you're describing is real though.** Pro players do have mental "playbooks." We can capture this by:
- Clustering replay openings into ~10-20 archetypal strategies per faction matchup
- Using these as soft conditioning signals during training
- The model learns "when I see pattern X in first 3 minutes, strategies like Y tend to win"

So: not fixed plans, but learned strategic awareness. Same result, much more flexible.

Does that address your concern, or do you want something more explicit?

## Section 1: System Architecture Overview

Six major components:

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                   ZH-COPILOT                        │
│                                                     │
│  ┌───────────┐  ┌───────────┐  ┌────────────────┐  │
│  │  Replay   │  │  Headless │  │   Screen       │  │
│  │  Parser   │  │  Engine   │  │   Capture      │  │
│  │  (Python) │  │  (C++)    │  │   Interface    │  │
│  └─────┬─────┘  └─────┬─────┘  └───────┬────────┘  │
│        │              │                 │           │
│        ▼              ▼                 ▼           │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │         Game State Abstraction Layer         │    │
│  │    (Unified representation for all inputs)   │    │
│  └──────────────────────┬──────────────────────┘    │
│                         │                           │
│                         ▼                           │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │              AI Model (PyTorch)              │    │
│  │  ┌─────────┐ ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐ ┌────────┐  │    │
│  │  │Strategy │ │Spatial│ │Entity│ │Temporal │  │    │
│  │  │Encoder  │ │CNN    │ │Trans.│ │LSTM    │  │    │
│  │  └────┬────┘ └──┬───┘ └──┬───┘ └───┬────┘  │    │
│  │       └─────────┴────────┴─────────┘        │    │
│  │                    │                         │    │
│  │              Action Decoder                  │    │
│  └──────────────────────┬──────────────────────┘    │
│                         │                           │
│                         ▼                           │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │           Action Execution Layer             │    │
│  │  Training: direct engine API                 │    │
│  │  Deploy:   keyboard/mouse emulation          │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                     │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │          Training Pipeline                   │    │
│  │  Phase 1: Imitation (replays)                │    │
│  │  Phase 2: RL vs built-in AI                  │    │
│  │  Phase 3: RL self-play                       │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Key principle:** The Game State Abstraction Layer is the critical boundary. Both the headless engine (training) and screen capture (deployment) produce the same unified state representation. The model never knows which input source it's using.

Does this top-level structure look right before I go deeper into each component?

## Section 2: Game State Representation

This is the most important design decision. The model sees the game through this representation.

**Three parallel inputs:**

### 2a. Spatial Map (CNN input)
Grid overlay on the game map. Multiple channels, like an image:

| Channel | Description |
|---------|-------------|
| Terrain | Passability, elevation, water |
| Own units | Unit type + health encoded per cell |
| Enemy units | Visible units (fog of war respected) |
| Buildings | Own + enemy structures |
| Resources | Supply docks, oil derricks |
| Fog of war | Explored / visible / hidden |
| Threat map | Estimated enemy DPS per cell |

Resolution: ~64x64 or 128x128 grid (tunable). Each cell covers a game-world area.

### 2b. Entity List (Transformer input)
Per-unit data for all visible entities:

```
[unit_type, faction, health%, x, y, 
 current_action, cooldowns, veterancy,
 is_garrisoned, carried_units, upgrades_applied]
```

Variable length — Transformer handles this naturally. Self-attention lets units "see" relationships between each other.

### 2c. Global Scalars (MLP input)
```
[money, power_surplus, supply_used, supply_cap,
 game_time, faction_id, opponent_faction_id,
 num_own_units, num_enemy_units_visible,
 tech_level, active_general_powers]
```

### 2d. Temporal Context (LSTM)
The LSTM carries forward:
- Scouting history (what was seen where, when)
- Opponent build patterns observed so far
- Own strategy trajectory

**Fog of war is critical.** The model only sees what a human player would see. No cheating. During training from engine we enforce visibility masks. During deployment, screen capture naturally provides only visible info.

How does this state representation look?

> DEVELOPER

is 128 enough? what if in 1 cell can conrain 2-3 units? and our and enemy?

> AGENT

Good catch. You're right — stacking units in a single cell loses information.

**Two solutions:**

### Option 1: Higher resolution (256x256 or 512x512)
- Fewer units per cell, less overlap
- But: 4-16x more memory/compute per forward pass
- Diminishing returns — units still overlap in choke points

### Option 2: Spatial map + Entity list work together (Recommended)

The spatial map doesn't need to resolve individual units. It provides **strategic overview** — where are clusters of force, threat zones, resource control. Think of it as a heatmap, not a unit tracker.

Individual unit tracking is the **Entity List's job**. Every unit has exact coordinates, health, type. The Transformer attends to each one individually. No information loss.

So the spatial map channels become:

| Channel | Encoding |
|---------|----------|
| Own unit density | Count of own units in cell (0, 1, 2, 3+) |
| Own unit DPS | Summed DPS of own units in cell |
| Own unit HP | Summed HP in cell |
| Enemy unit density | Same for enemy |
| Enemy unit DPS | Same |
| Enemy unit HP | Same |
| Buildings | Type encoded |
| Terrain/fog | Same as before |

Cell has 3 own units and 2 enemy? Spatial map says "dense contested area, our DPS = X, their DPS = Y." The entity list knows exactly which units, their health, cooldowns, everything.

**128x128 is fine with this design.** The spatial CNN reads the macro picture, the Transformer reads micro detail. They fuse in the final layers.

This is what AlphaStar did — spatial features for strategy, entity list for tactics.

Good with this?

> DEVELOPER

ok go

> AGENT

## Section 3: Action Space

The model outputs a **hierarchical action** each decision step (~200ms game time, 5 actions/sec).

### Action structure (decoded sequentially):

```
1. Action Type    → what to do (21 types)
2. Target Unit    → pointer into entity list (if applicable)  
3. Target Position → x,y on map (if applicable)
4. Modifier       → action-specific params
```

### Action types:

| Category | Actions |
|----------|---------|
| **Economy** | Build structure, train unit, sell building, set rally point |
| **Movement** | Move, attack-move, patrol, guard, enter garrison |
| **Combat** | Attack target, force-fire position, retreat, use ability |
| **Tech** | Research upgrade, choose general power, activate power |
| **Strategy** | Select group, set waypoint, no-op (wait) |

### Key design decisions:

**Pointer network for target selection.** Instead of discretizing "which unit to target" into a fixed vocabulary, the model attends over the entity list and picks one. Handles any number of units on screen.

**Spatial output for positions.** A 64x64 probability map (upsampled from the CNN) for "where on the map." Used for movement, building placement, ability targeting.

**Auto-regressive decoding.** Action type is sampled first, then conditions the target/position heads. Not all heads fire — "train unit" needs no target position, "move" needs no target unit.

**Multi-action batching.** Real players issue multiple commands between decision steps (shift-click waypoints, control groups). The model can output 1-3 actions per step via a repeat head.

**No-op is important.** Sometimes the right move is to do nothing. The model must learn this — constant fidgeting is a real failure mode in RL-trained RTS agents.

Does this action space make sense?

> DEVELOPER

ok

> AGENT

## Section 4: Model Architecture

```
                    Game State Input
                         │
          ┌──────────────┼──────────────┐
          ▼              ▼              ▼
   ┌────────────┐ ┌────────────┐ ┌──────────┐
   │ Spatial Map│ │ Entity List│ │  Global  │
   │  128x128   │ │  N×feature │ │  Scalars │
   │  ~12 chan  │ │    dim     │ │   ~20    │
   └─────┬──────┘ └─────┬──────┘ └────┬─────┘
         ▼              ▼              ▼
   ┌────────────┐ ┌────────────┐ ┌──────────┐
   │ ResNet-18  │ │ Transformer│ │ MLP      │
   │ (modified) │ │ 6 layers   │ │ 2 layers │
   │ → 256 feat │ │ 8 heads    │ │ → 128    │
   └─────┬──────┘ └─────┬──────┘ └────┬─────┘
         │              │              │
         └──────────────┼──────────────┘
                        ▼
                 ┌─────────────┐
                 │   Fusion    │
                 │  MLP 512    │
                 └──────┬──────┘
                        ▼
                 ┌─────────────┐
                 │   LSTM      │
                 │  512 hidden │
                 └──────┬──────┘
                        │
          ┌─────────────┼─────────────┐
          ▼             ▼             ▼
   ┌────────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐
   │  Strategy  │ │  Policy  │ │  Value   │
   │  Encoder   │ │  Head    │ │  Head    │
   │  → 64 dim  │ │(actions) │ │(win prob)│
   └────────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘
                       │
            ┌──────────┼──────────┐
            ▼          ▼          ▼
       Action Type  Target    Position
       (softmax)   (pointer)  (spatial)
```

**Size estimates:**
- ~15-25M parameters total
- Inference: ~10-20ms on M4 (well within 200ms budget)
- Memory: ~100MB model weight

**Why these choices:**
- **ResNet-18** not deeper — spatial map is low-res, don't need ResNet-50
- **6-layer Transformer** — enough for ~200 entities. ZH rarely has 500+ units on screen
- **LSTM over GRU** — slightly better at long sequences, negligible cost difference
- **Separate value head** — standard actor-critic for RL training
- **Strategy encoder** — feeds back into policy, also used for opponent strategy clustering

**Training modes:**
- **Imitation:** Policy head trains via cross-entropy against human actions from replays
- **RL:** Policy + Value heads train via PPO against game outcomes

This is deliberately compact. Bigger isn't better for RTS — inference speed matters. We can scale up later if needed.

How's this look?

> DEVELOPER

i have questions for resnet we have200 entities which entities?

> AGENT

ResNet processes the **spatial map**, not entities. The Transformer handles entities.

**What counts as an entity:**

Every individual game object on the map that has gameplay relevance:

| Category | Examples |
|----------|---------|
| **Combat units** | Tank, Humvee, MiG, Quad Cannon, Technical, Overlord... |
| **Infantry** | Ranger, Red Guard, Rebel, RPG Trooper (each soldier, not squad) |
| **Buildings** | Command Center, Barracks, War Factory, Supply Center... |
| **Defenses** | Patriot Missile, Gattling Cannon, Stinger Site... |
| **Resource** | Supply trucks, Supply docks, Oil derricks |
| **Projectiles/Effects** | No — too noisy, low value |
| **Neutral** | Tech buildings, civilian structures (if capturable) |

**~200 is the typical upper bound for a mid-late game 1v1.** Could reach 300-400 in large team games. The Transformer handles variable length natively — no problem.

**Per squad question:** ZH infantry come in squads (e.g., 5 Red Guard = 1 "unit" in game terms). We model them as **one entity per squad**, not per soldier. The entity features include squad size / HP remaining.

Clear?

> DEVELOPER

lets have 500.. and mb more layers..what is the option along with resnet18

> AGENT

500 entities is safe for team games / FFA. Transformer scales linearly with layers, quadratically with sequence length — 500 is fine.

**Updated Transformer:** 8 layers, 8 heads, 256 dim. Handles 500 entities comfortably.

**For the spatial encoder, options:**

| Option | Params | Speed | Quality |
|--------|--------|-------|---------|
| ResNet-18 | 11M | Fast | Good enough |
| ResNet-34 | 21M | Moderate | Marginal gain for low-res input |
| **EfficientNet-B2** | 9M | Fast | Better feature extraction per param |
| ConvNeXt-Tiny | 28M | Slower | Overkill |
| Custom lightweight CNN | 3-5M | Fastest | Fully tuned to our input |

**My recommendation: Custom CNN.**

Here's why — ResNet/EfficientNet are designed for natural images (224x224 RGB, textures, edges, objects). Our input is a 128x128 **engineered feature map** with 12 clean channels. It's more like a satellite heatmap than a photo.

A purpose-built CNN with:
- 6 conv blocks with residual connections
- Progressive downsampling: 128→64→32→16→8→4
- ~4M params
- Channel attention (squeeze-excitation) to weight which channels matter

Faster inference, fewer wasted parameters, designed for exactly our input format.

**Updated model total:** ~20-30M params. Still runs <20ms on M4.

Good with custom CNN + 8-layer Transformer for 500 entities?

> DEVELOPER

lets try, but lets have model be ready for re-training for ffa  team games

> AGENT

Right. So the architecture needs to handle:

- **1v1:** ~200 entities, 2 players
- **2v2-4v4:** ~500-800 entities, 4-8 players  
- **FFA:** ~400-600 entities, 3-8 players

**What changes for multi-player:**

### Entity list
- Add `player_id` and `alliance` fields to each entity
- Transformer attention naturally scales to 800+ (just slower)
- **Hard cap at 1024 entities** — if exceeded, prioritize by proximity + threat level, drop least relevant

### Global scalars
- Per-player info becomes a list: `[player_1_money, player_1_units, ..., player_N_money, ...]`
- Fixed slot size for 8 players, pad with zeros for smaller games
- Add `game_mode` flag (1v1, 2v2, 3v3, 4v4, FFA)
- Add `team_id` per player slot

### Spatial map
- Instead of 2-channel (own/enemy), use **8-channel per attribute** (one per player slot)
- Own units density, Player 2 density, ... Player 8 density
- Increases spatial channels from ~12 to ~40
- Custom CNN handles this — just adjust input channels

### Action space
- No change. You only control your own units. Actions are the same regardless of mode.

### Strategy encoder
- Must learn per-opponent embeddings, not just "the enemy"
- In FFA: track threat level per player, shifting alliances

**The model is the same architecture.** Input dimensions are parameterized:

```python
config = {
    "max_players": 8,
    "max_entities": 1024,
    "spatial_channels": 40,  # scales with max_players
    "spatial_resolution": 128,
    "game_modes": ["1v1", "2v2", "3v3", "4v4", "ffa"]
}
```

Train Phase 1 on 1v1 with most slots empty. Phase 2+ fills them in. Same weights, just seeing more of the input space.

Good?

> DEVELOPER

we can start training eve on 1v1 maps i think< or it's bad?

> AGENT

Not bad at all. Smart actually.

**Why 1v1 first is the right call:**
- Simpler state — model learns fundamentals (build orders, micro, macro) without multi-player noise
- Faster training — fewer entities, faster game simulation
- More replay data available — 1v1 is the most played competitive mode
- Core skills transfer — unit control, economy management, scouting all carry over

**What transfers to team/FFA later:**
- Unit micro (90% identical)
- Build orders and economy (80%)
- Map awareness and scouting (70%)

**What doesn't transfer and needs retraining:**
- Team coordination (new skill entirely)
- FFA diplomacy / threat balancing (new)
- Multi-front defense (partially new)
- Resource sharing in team games

**The plan:**
1. Train on 1v1 until strong
2. Freeze lower layers (CNN, entity encoder)
3. Fine-tune upper layers (LSTM, strategy encoder, policy head) on team/FFA data
4. Unfreeze all layers for final polish

This is standard transfer learning. The model doesn't lose 1v1 skills — it builds on them.

Good. Ready for the next design section?

> DEVELOPER

> AGENT

## Section 5: Training Pipeline

### Phase 1: Imitation Learning (Replays)

```
.rep files → Replay Parser → State-Action pairs → Supervised training
```

**Replay Parser:**
- Reads .rep files (command logs, not game state)
- Replays commands through headless engine to reconstruct full game state at each tick
- Outputs: `(state_t, action_t, player_id, outcome)` pairs
- Stores as HDF5 or Arrow/Parquet datasets for fast loading

**Training:**
- Loss: cross-entropy on action type + pointer loss on target + spatial cross-entropy on position
- Auxiliary losses: value head predicts game outcome, strategy encoder predicts opponent's next action
- ~50K replays × ~2000 decisions per game = ~100M training samples
- Estimate: 3-5 days on M4 for convergence

**Expected result:** Bot that plays like an average human. Knows build orders, basic micro, doesn't do anything stupid. ~40-50% win rate vs built-in Hard AI.

---

### Phase 2: RL vs Built-in AI

```
Headless Engine ←→ pybind11 ←→ PyTorch Agent ←→ PPO update
```

**Setup:**
- Headless engine runs on Intel Mac (or cloud)
- Agent plays full games vs built-in AI (Easy → Medium → Hard → Brutal)
- Multiple parallel environments for throughput

**Reward shaping:**

| Signal | Weight | Why |
|--------|--------|-----|
| Win/loss | 1.0 | Ultimate objective |
| Resource collected | 0.01 | Encourages economy |
| Units killed value | 0.05 | Encourages cost-effective trades |
| Units lost value | -0.03 | Punishes waste |
| Map control (%) | 0.02 | Encourages expansion/scouting |
| Tech progression | 0.01 | Encourages teching up |

Rewards decay over training — eventually only win/loss matters. Early shaping prevents the RL agent from discovering degenerate strategies.

**Expected result:** Beats Brutal AI consistently. ~60% vs average humans.

---

### Phase 3: Self-play

```
Current agent vs Past versions of itself (league training)
```

**League system (simplified AlphaStar league):**
- **Main agent** — always training, plays against exploiters + past selves
- **Past snapshots** — frozen checkpoints saved every N games
- **Exploiters** — agents specifically trained to beat the main agent's weaknesses

**Why league matters:** Without it, self-play collapses into rock-paper-scissors cycles. Agent learns to beat version N, forgets how to beat version N-1. League forces robustness.

**Expected result:** Beats good-to-top human players. 70%+ win rate.

---

### Hardware allocation:

| Machine | Phase 1 | Phase 2 | Phase 3 |
|---------|---------|---------|---------|
| **M4 Mac** | Model training | Model training | Model training |
| **Intel Mac** | Replay → state reconstruction | Run headless engine(s) | Run headless engine(s) |

**Communication:** Intel Mac runs game envs, serves state/reward over local network or ZeroMQ. M4 Mac runs the model and sends actions back.

How's the training pipeline?

> DEVELOPER

good...but question...do we have both macs running at the same time? does it mean model and trainng on m4, intel mac runs game? and we sync them on the fly?

> AGENT

Yes. Two machines working in parallel, communicating over local network.

```
┌─────────────────┐         ZeroMQ          ┌─────────────────┐
│   Intel Mac     │ ◄─────────────────────► │    M4 Mac       │
│                 │    local network/WiFi    │                 │
│  Headless game  │                         │  PyTorch model  │
│  engine(s)      │  ── state observation → │  PPO trainer    │
│  (C++ process)  │  ← action ──────────── │  GPU (MPS)      │
│                 │  ── reward, done ─────→ │                 │
│  4-8 parallel   │                         │  Batch updates  │
│  game instances │                         │  every N steps  │
└─────────────────┘                         └─────────────────┘
```

**Flow per step:**
1. Intel Mac: engine advances game tick → extracts state → sends to M4
2. M4: model forward pass → picks action → sends back to Intel
3. Intel Mac: executes action in engine → next tick
4. M4: accumulates experience buffer → runs PPO update every ~2048 steps

**Latency concern:** Over WiFi, round trip ~1-5ms. Game tick is ~200ms. No bottleneck.

**But there's a simpler alternative for Phase 1 (imitation learning):**

No live sync needed. Intel Mac pre-processes all replays into datasets offline. Copy files to M4. Train purely locally. Two machines only need to sync during Phase 2+.

**And honestly** — for Phase 2, you could also run everything on the Intel Mac alone. 16GB is tight but:
- Headless engine: ~200MB per instance × 4 = 800MB
- PyTorch model + training: ~2-4GB
- Total: ~5GB. Fits in 16GB.

Training would be slower (no MPS GPU), but simpler setup. No networking code.

**Three options:**

| Setup | Complexity | Speed | When to use |
|-------|-----------|-------|-------------|
| **Intel only** | Simplest | Slow | Early prototyping |
| **Both synced** | Moderate | Fast | Phase 2-3 production training |
| **Cloud GPU + Intel** | Most complex | Fastest | If M4 becomes bottleneck |

I'd say: **start with Intel-only for Phase 1-2, add M4 sync when you need speed in Phase 3.**

Agree?

I have all the information needed. Here is the summary:

---

## C&C Generals Zero Hour: Game Tick Rate / Simulation Speed

### Definitive answer from the source code

The authoritative definition is in **`GameCommon.h`** (both `Generals/` and `GeneralsMD/` directories):

**File:** `/tmp/CnC_Generals_Zero_Hour/GeneralsMD/Code/GameEngine/Include/Common/GameCommon.h` (lines 70-78)

```cpp
enum
{
    LOGICFRAMES_PER_SECOND = 30,
    MSEC_PER_SECOND = 1000
};
const Real LOGICFRAMES_PER_MSEC_REAL = (((Real)LOGICFRAMES_PER_SECOND) / ((Real)MSEC_PER_SECOND));
const Real MSEC_PER_LOGICFRAME_REAL = (((Real)MSEC_PER_SECOND) / ((Real)LOGICFRAMES_PER_SECOND));
const Real LOGICFRAMES_PER_SECONDS_REAL = (Real)LOGICFRAMES_PER_SECOND;
const Real SECONDS_PER_LOGICFRAME_REAL = 1.0f / LOGICFRAMES_PER_SECONDS_REAL;
```

### Key constants

| Constant | Value | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `LOGICFRAMES_PER_SECOND` | **30** | Game simulation runs at 30 logic frames per second |
| `MSEC_PER_LOGICFRAME_REAL` | **33.333...** | Each logic frame is ~33.33 milliseconds |
| `SECONDS_PER_LOGICFRAME_REAL` | **0.0333...** | Each logic frame is 1/30th of a second |
| `DEFAULT_MAX_FPS` | **45** | Default render frame rate cap (from `GameEngine.h` line 37) |

### Additional details

- **`TheW3DFrameLengthInMsec`** is initialized to `1000/LOGICFRAMES_PER_SECOND` (= 33ms), described in the code as "default is 33msec/frame == 30fps. but we may change it depending on sys config."

- The logic update rate is **fixed at 30 FPS** and is decoupled from the render rate. The render rate defaults to 45 FPS (`DEFAULT_MAX_FPS`) but can be overridden via command line.

- In multiplayer, the network code caps FPS to `m_framesPerSecondLimit` with a comment "Cap to 30 FPS" in `ConnectionManager.cpp`.

- The game speed slider in the skirmish options menu ranges from 15 to 61 FPS (`max(15, min(61, ...))` in `SkirmishGameOptionsMenu.cpp`), which controls `m_framesPerSecondLimit` -- but the core simulation tick is always **30 logic frames per second**.

- The `setTimeMultiplier` on the view is used for visual effects (camera animations etc.), not the logic tick rate.

### Summary

**The SAGE engine (used by C&C Generals and Zero Hour) runs game logic at a fixed 30 ticks per second (30 Hz), with each tick being exactly 33.33 milliseconds.** This is the fundamental simulation rate used for all game logic, AI, physics, and networking.

> AGENT

I was wrong. Not 200ms.

**Actual tick rate: 30 logic frames per second = ~33ms per tick.**

That's 6x faster than I claimed. This changes the design:

**Impact:**
- 30 decisions/sec is too fast — no human plays that way, and model inference adds up
- **We should decide every N ticks, not every tick**
- Reasonable: decide every 5-10 ticks = **3-6 decisions/sec** (~170-330ms between decisions)
- AlphaStar used ~22 non-duplicate actions per 5 seconds (~4.4/sec) — same ballpark

**Updated decision rate: 5 actions/sec (every 6 ticks, ~200ms).** Configurable.

Good catch. Continuing to next section?

> DEVELOPER

lets do 3 actions per second

> AGENT

3 actions/sec. Every 10 ticks (~333ms). Reasonable — close to human APM for strategic play.

That's ~180 APM equivalent. Pro players peak higher in micro bursts but average around 150-250 APM. Fits well.

Next section.

## Section 6: Deployment — Copilot & Autopilot

```
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                  ZH-COPILOT APP                  │
│                                                  │
│  ┌────────────┐    ┌───────────────────────────┐ │
│  │  Screen    │    │   State Recognizer (CV)   │ │
│  │  Capture   │───►│  - Unit detection (YOLO)  │ │
│  │  (60 FPS)  │    │  - Minimap parser         │ │
│  │            │    │  - Resource/UI OCR         │ │
│  └────────────┘    │  - Building recognition   │ │
│                    └─────────────┬─────────────┘ │
│                                  ▼               │
│                    ┌───────────────────────────┐ │
│                    │  Game State Abstraction   │ │
│                    │  (same format as training)│ │
│                    └─────────────┬─────────────┘ │
│                                  ▼               │
│                    ┌───────────────────────────┐ │
│                    │  AI Model (inference)     │ │
│                    └─────────────┬─────────────┘ │
│                                  ▼               │
│                    ┌───────────────────────────┐ │
│                    │     Mode Switch           │ │
│                    ├──────────┬────────────────┤ │
│                    │ COPILOT  │   AUTOPILOT    │ │
│                    │          │                │ │
│                    │ Overlay  │ Keyboard +     │ │
│                    │ showing  │ Mouse          │ │
│                    │ suggested│ emulation      │ │
│                    │ actions  │ (pyautogui)    │ │
│                    └──────────┴────────────────┘ │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Screen → State is the hard part.** This is essentially a second ML problem:

| Component | Approach |
|-----------|----------|
| Unit detection | Fine-tuned YOLOv8 on ZH screenshots |
| Unit type classification | CNN classifier on cropped sprites |
| Minimap parsing | Color segmentation + known map layouts |
| Resources/UI | OCR (Tesseract or small custom model) |
| Health bars | Color + position heuristic |
| Fog of war | Pixel brightness thresholding |

**Training data for CV models:** Run games in headless engine, render frames, auto-label from known game state. Free labeled data.

**Copilot mode overlay:**
- Transparent overlay window on top of game
- Shows: suggested build order, attack arrows on minimap, unit selection highlights
- Human decides whether to follow suggestions

**Autopilot mode:**
- Translates model actions → mouse clicks + keyboard shortcuts
- Needs action-to-input mapping: "build tank" → click War Factory → click Tank icon (or hotkey)
- Camera management: model must also decide where to look

**Gap between training and deployment:** The CV pipeline will lose some information vs direct engine state. Model needs to be robust to:
- Misclassified units
- Units missed in dense fights
- Slight state lag from capture pipeline

We mitigate by adding noise during training — randomly drop/misclassify entities to simulate CV errors.

Good with this deployment design?

> DEVELOPER

i'd say copiot mode - ai bot plays - i'm giving tips

> AGENT

Ha, reversed from what I assumed.

So:
- **Autopilot** = bot plays fully autonomous (primary mode)
- **Copilot** = bot plays, human gives strategic tips/overrides

Examples of human tips:
- "Rush him now"
- "Go air"
- "Expand left"
- "He's teching, punish"

**How to input tips:**
- A) **Hotkey presets** — F1=rush, F2=defend, F3=expand, F4=go air, etc. Simple, fast, limited.
- B) **Text chat** — type commands in an overlay. Flexible but slow mid-game.
- C) **Click-based** — click on minimap to say "attack here" / "defend here" + modifier key for intent. Fast and spatial.
- D) **Voice** — speak commands, speech-to-text → parsed to strategy signals. Hands-free.

**My recommendation: A + C combined.** Hotkeys for strategic shifts, click+modifier for spatial directives. Fast enough for real-time play. Voice as a later nice-to-have.

**Implementation:** Tips feed into the strategy encoder as an additional conditioning signal. The model's policy shifts toward the human's directive without blindly following it — if human says "rush" but model sees it's suicidal, it tempers the aggression.

Which input method(s) do you want?

> DEVELOPER

ui with buttons, where we will have deffence/atack/ rush/ farm/turtle, and voice input latter

**Strategy buttons:**

| Button | Effect on strategy encoder |
|--------|---------------------------|
| Attack | Bias toward aggression |
| Defend | Hold positions, build defenses |
| Rush | All-in early pressure |
| Farm | Prioritize economy, expand |
| Turtle | Max defenses, tech up |
| Auto | No human override, model decides |

Default state: **Auto**. Human clicks a button to nudge the model. Can switch mid-game.

Encoded as a one-hot vector appended to global scalars. Simple.

{
  "file_path": "/Users/4thwithme/Documents/repo/zh-copilot/docs/specs/2026-03-30-zh-copilot-design.md",
  "content": "# ZH-Copilot: AI Bot for C&C Generals Zero Hour\n\n**Date:** 2026-03-30\n**Status:** Design approved, pending implementation plan\n\n---\n\n## 1. Overview\n\nZH-Copilot is an AI bot that plays Command & Conquer: Generals Zero Hour at a competitive level. It operates in two modes:\n\n- **Autopilot:** Bot plays fully autonomously. Human starts the game and hands off control.\n- **Copilot:** Bot plays, human provides strategic guidance via UI buttons (Attack, Defend, Rush, Farm, Turtle, Auto).\n\n**Target:** 70%+ win rate against good-to-top human players. All three factions (USA, China, GLA). All official maps.\n\n**Deployment:** Screen capture + keyboard/mouse emulation. No game modification required.\n\n**Training:** Direct engine integration via headless build of the open-source EA codebase.\n\n---\n\n## 2. System Architecture\n\n```\n┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐\n│                   ZH-COPILOT                        │\n│                                                     │\n│  ┌───────────┐  ┌───────────┐  ┌────────────────┐  │\n│  │  Replay   │  │  Headless │  │   Screen       │  │\n│  │  Parser   │  │  Engine   │  │   Capture      │  │\n│  │  (Python) │  │  (C++)    │  │   Interface    │  │\n│  └─────┬─────┘  └─────┬─────┘  └───────┬────────┘  │\n│        │              │                 │           │\n│        ▼              ▼                 ▼           │\n│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │\n│  │         Game State Abstraction Layer         │    │\n│  │    (Unified representation for all inputs)   │    │\n│  └──────────────────────┬──────────────────────┘    │\n│                         │                           │\n│                         ▼                           │\n│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │\n│  │              AI Model (PyTorch)              │    │\n│  │  ┌─────────┐ ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐ ┌────────┐  │    │\n│  │  │Strategy │ │Custom│ │Entity│ │Temporal │  │    │\n│  │  │Encoder  │ │ CNN  │ │Trans.│ │LSTM    │  │    │\n│  │  └────┬────┘ └──┬───┘ └──┬───┘ └───┬────┘  │    │\n│  │       └─────────┴────────┴─────────┘        │    │\n│  │                    │                         │    │\n│  │              Action Decoder                  │    │\n│  └──────────────────────┬──────────────────────┘    │\n│                         │                           │\n│                         ▼                           │\n│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │\n│  │           Action Execution Layer             │    │\n│  │  Training: direct engine API                 │    │\n│  │  Deploy:   keyboard/mouse emulation          │    │\n│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │\n└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘\n```\n\n**Key principle:** The Game State Abstraction Layer is the critical boundary. Both the headless engine (training) and screen capture (deployment) produce the same unified state representation. The model never knows which input source it's using.\n\n---\n\n## 3. Game State Representation\n\nThree parallel inputs consumed by the model every decision step.\n\n### 3.1 Spatial Map (Custom CNN input)\n\n128x128 grid overlay on the game map. ~40 channels to support up to 8 players.\n\nPer-player channels (×8 player slots):\n- Unit density (count per cell)\n- Unit DPS (summed)\n- Unit HP (summed)\n\nShared channels:\n- Buildings (type encoded per player)\n- Terrain (passability, elevation, water)\n- Fog of war (explored / visible / hidden)\n- Resource locations (supply docks, oil derricks)\n- Threat heatmap\n\nFor 1v1, 6 of 8 player slots are zeroed. Architecture supports all modes without modification.\n\n### 3.2 Entity List (Transformer input)\n\nPer-entity feature vector for all visible game objects (max 1024 entities):\n\n```\n[entity_id, unit_type, faction, player_id, alliance,\n health%, x, y, current_action, cooldowns, veterancy,\n is_garrisoned, squad_size, carried_units, upgrades_applied]\n```\n\nPriority system when entity count exceeds 1024: sort by proximity + threat level, drop least relevant.\n\n### 3.3 Global Scalars (MLP input)\n\nPer-player slots (×8):\n```\n[money, power_surplus, supply_used, supply_cap,\n num_units, tech_level, faction_id, team_id]\n```\n\nGame-level:\n```\n[game_time, game_mode, map_id,\n human_strategy_override]  // from copilot UI buttons\n```\n\n### 3.4 Temporal Context (LSTM)\n\nThe LSTM hidden state carries forward:\n- Scouting history\n- Opponent build patterns\n- Own strategy trajectory\n- Game phase awareness\n\n---\n\n## 4. Action Space\n\nDecision rate: **3 actions per second** (every 10 game ticks at 30 ticks/sec, ~333ms).\n\n### 4.1 Hierarchical Action Structure\n\nEach decision step outputs:\n1. **Action Type** — what to do (softmax over ~21 types)\n2. **Target Unit** — pointer into entity list (pointer network)\n3. **Target Position** — x,y on map (64x64 spatial probability map)\n4. **Modifier** — action-specific parameters\n\n### 4.2 Action Types\n\n| Category | Actions |\n|----------|---------|\n| Economy | Build structure, train unit, sell building, set rally point |\n| Movement | Move, attack-move, patrol, guard, enter garrison |\n| Combat | Attack target, force-fire position, retreat, use ability |\n| Tech | Research upgrade, choose general power, activate power |\n| Strategy | Select group, set waypoint, no-op (wait) |\n\n### 4.3 Design Decisions\n\n- **Pointer network** for target selection — attends over entity list, handles any unit count.\n- **Spatial output** for positions — 64x64 probability map, used for movement, building placement, ability targeting.\n- **Auto-regressive decoding** — action type sampled first, conditions subsequent heads. Not all heads fire per action.\n- **Multi-action batching** — model can output 1-3 actions per step via repeat head.\n- **No-op is critical** — the model must learn when doing nothing is optimal.\n\n---\n\n## 5. Model Architecture\n\n### 5.1 Architecture Diagram\n\n```\n                    Game State Input\n                         |\n          +--------------+--------------+\n          v              v              v\n   +------------+ +------------+ +----------+\n   | Spatial Map| | Entity List| |  Global  |\n   |  128x128   | |  N x feat  | |  Scalars |\n   |  ~40 chan  | |   dim      | |   ~80    |\n   +-----+------+ +-----+------+ +----+-----+\n         v              v              v\n   +------------+ +------------+ +----------+\n   | Custom CNN | | Transformer| | MLP      |\n   | 6 blocks   | | 8 layers   | | 2 layers |\n   | ResidConv  | | 8 heads    | | -> 128   |\n   | SE-attn    | | 256 dim    | |          |\n   | -> 256 feat| | max 1024   | |          |\n   +-----+------+ +-----+------+ +----+-----+\n         |              |              |\n         +--------------+--------------+\n                        v\n                 +-------------+\n                 |   Fusion    |\n                 |  MLP 512    |\n                 +------+------+\n                        v\n                 +-------------+\n                 |   LSTM      |\n                 |  512 hidden |\n                 +------+------+\n                        |\n          +-------------+-------------+\n          v             v             v\n   +------------+ +----------+ +----------+\n   |  Strategy  | |  Policy  | |  Value   |\n   |  Encoder   | |  Head    | |  Head    |\n   |  -> 64 dim | | (actions)| | (win %)  |\n   +------------+ +----------+ +----------+\n                       |\n            +----------+----------+\n            v          v          v\n       Action Type  Target    Position\n       (softmax)   (pointer)  (spatial)\n```\n\n### 5.2 Component Details\n\n**Custom CNN (Spatial Encoder):**\n- 6 convolutional blocks with residual connections\n- Progressive downsampling: 128 -> 64 -> 32 -> 16 -> 8 -> 4\n- Squeeze-excitation attention per block\n- ~4M parameters\n- Purpose-built for engineered feature maps, not natural images\n\n**Transformer (Entity Encoder):**\n- 8 layers, 8 attention heads, 256 embedding dim\n- Handles up to 1024 entities\n- Self-attention captures unit-to-unit relationships\n- ~8M parameters\n\n**LSTM (Temporal Module):**\n- 512 hidden units\n- Carries game history context across decision steps\n- Enables strategic planning and adaptation\n\n**Strategy Encoder:**\n- Outputs 64-dim continuous strategy embedding\n- Learned archetypes from replay clustering (~10-20 per faction matchup)\n- Receives human override signal from copilot UI buttons\n- Feeds into policy head as conditioning\n\n**Value Head:**\n- Predicts win probability\n- Used for RL training (actor-critic)\n- Separate from policy to allow independent learning rates\n\n### 5.3 Model Size\n\n- Total: ~20-30M parameters\n- Inference: <20ms on M4 Mac (well within 333ms budget)\n- Memory: ~100MB model weights\n\n---\n\n## 6. Training Pipeline\n\n### 6.1 Phase 1: Imitation Learning (Replays)\n\n**Pipeline:** `.rep files -> Replay Parser -> Headless Engine -> State-Action pairs -> Supervised training`\n\n**Replay Parser:**\n- Reads .rep files (command logs)\n- Replays commands through headless engine to reconstruct full game state\n- Outputs (state_t, action_t, player_id, outcome) tuples\n- Stores as HDF5 or Parquet datasets\n\n**Training:**\n- Loss: cross-entropy on action type + pointer loss on target + spatial CE on position\n- Auxiliary losses: value head predicts game outcome, strategy encoder predicts opponent actions\n- Data: ~10K-50K replays x ~600 decisions/game (3/sec x ~200sec avg) = 6M-30M samples\n- Estimate: 3-5 days on M4 for convergence\n\n**Expected result:** Bot plays like average human. Knows build orders, basic micro. ~40-50% vs Hard AI.\n\n**Strategy Clustering:**\n- Cluster replay openings into ~10-20 archetypes per faction matchup\n- Label replays with strategy embeddings\n- Soft conditioning — model blends strategies, not fixed plans\n\n### 6.2 Phase 2: RL vs Built-in AI\n\n**Setup:**\n- Headless engine on Intel Mac (4-8 parallel instances)\n- Agent plays full games vs built-in AI\n- Curriculum: Easy -> Medium -> Hard -> Brutal\n\n**Reward Shaping:**\n\n| Signal | Weight | Decays |\n|--------|--------|--------|\n| Win/loss | 1.0 | No |\n| Resources collected | 0.01 | Yes |\n| Units killed (value) | 0.05 | Yes |\n| Units lost (value) | -0.03 | Yes |\n| Map control % | 0.02 | Yes |\n| Tech progression | 0.01 | Yes |\n\nShaped rewards decay over training. Eventually only win/loss matters.\n\n**Algorithm:** PPO (Proximal Policy Optimization)\n- Batch size: 2048 steps\n- Multiple parallel environments for throughput\n\n**Expected result:** Beats Brutal AI consistently. ~60% vs average humans.\n\n### 6.3 Phase 3: Self-play League\n\n**League System (simplified AlphaStar):**\n- **Main agent** — always training, plays against exploiters + past selves\n- **Past snapshots** — frozen checkpoints saved every N games\n- **Exploiters** — agents trained to beat main agent's weaknesses\n\n**Why league:** Prevents rock-paper-scissors cycles in self-play. Forces robustness across strategies.\n\n**ELO tracking** for all agents. Matchmaking weighted toward close-ELO opponents.\n\n**Expected result:** 70%+ win rate vs good-to-top humans.\n\n### 6.4 Hardware Setup\n\n**Phase 1 (imitation):**\n- Intel Mac: pre-process replays into datasets offline\n- M4 Mac: train model locally (no live sync needed)\n\n**Phase 2-3 (RL):**\n- Start with Intel Mac only (engine + training, simpler)\n- Scale to dual-machine setup if speed becomes bottleneck:\n\n```\nIntel Mac (engine)  <--ZeroMQ-->  M4 Mac (model/training)\n  state ->                          <- actions\n  reward ->                         <- PPO updates\n```\n\n---\n\n## 7. Deployment\n\n### 7.1 Screen Capture Pipeline\n\n```\nScreen Capture (60 FPS)\n        |\n        v\nState Recognizer (CV)\n  - Unit detection: YOLOv8 fine-tuned on ZH screenshots\n  - Unit type classification: CNN on cropped sprites\n  - Minimap parsing: color segmentation + known layouts\n  - Resource/UI: OCR\n  - Health bars: color + position heuristic\n  - Fog of war: pixel brightness thresholding\n        |\n        v\nGame State Abstraction (same format as training)\n        |\n        v\nAI Model (inference)\n        |\n        v\nAction Execution (keyboard/mouse emulation)\n```\n\n**CV training data:** Render frames from headless engine with known game state = free labeled data.\n\n**Robustness:** Add noise during RL training (randomly drop/misclassify entities) to simulate CV errors at deployment.\n\n### 7.2 Input Emulation\n\n- Action-to-input mapping: \"build tank\" -> click War Factory -> Tank hotkey\n- Camera management: model decides where to look\n- Control group management: model manages unit selections\n- Implementation: pyautogui or platform-native input library\n\n### 7.3 Copilot UI\n\n**Strategy Buttons:**\n\n| Button | Effect |\n|--------|--------|\n| Attack | Bias toward aggression |\n| Defend | Hold positions, build defenses |\n| Rush | All-in early pressure |\n| Farm | Prioritize economy, expand |\n| Turtle | Max defenses, tech up |\n| Auto | No override, model decides (default) |\n\nEncoded as one-hot vector in global scalars. Model's policy shifts toward human directive without blindly following it.\n\n**Voice input:** Future addition. Speech-to-text -> command parsing -> map to strategy signals.\n\n---\n\n## 8. Phased Rollout\n\n| Phase | Scope | Success Criteria |\n|-------|-------|-----------------|\n| 1v1 Tournament Maps | Core training, single opponent | Beat Brutal AI |\n| 1v1 All Official Maps | Generalize across maps | Maintain win rate |\n| Team Games (2v2-4v4) | Fine-tune for multi-player | Competitive in team |\n| FFA | Multi-opponent adaptation | Competitive in FFA |\n\nArchitecture supports all modes from day one (8 player slots, variable entity count). Training scope expands per phase.\n\n**Transfer learning approach:**\n1. Train on 1v1 until strong\n2. Freeze lower layers (CNN, entity encoder)\n3. Fine-tune upper layers (LSTM, strategy encoder, policy) on team/FFA\n4. Unfreeze all for final polish\n\n---\n\n## 9. Key Technical Risks\n\n| Risk | Impact | Mitigation |\n|------|--------|------------|\n| Engine porting (stripping DirectX/Win32) | Blocks all training | Start here. Validate early. |\n| Replay format parsing | Blocks Phase 1 | Community may have parsers. Fallback: train from self-play only. |\n| Screen capture CV accuracy | Degrades deployment | Train with noise injection. Build robust CV pipeline. |\n| RL training instability | Slow progress | Careful reward shaping. Start from strong imitation baseline. |\n| Compute limitations (16GB Intel Mac) | Limits parallelism | Start with fewer envs. Scale to cloud if needed. |\n| Game tick rate mismatch training vs deploy | Bot behaves differently | Standardize decision rate (3/sec) across both. |\n\n---\n\n## 10. Technology Stack\n\n| Component | Technology |\n|-----------|-----------|\n| ML Framework | PyTorch |\n| Game Engine | C++ (EA open-source, GPL v3) |\n| Python Bindings | pybind11 |\n| RL Algorithm | PPO |\n| Object Detection | YOLOv8 |\n| OCR | Tesseract / custom |\n| Input Emulation | pyautogui |\n| IPC (dual machine) | ZeroMQ |\n| Data Storage | HDF5 / Parquet |\n| Experiment Tracking | W&B or TensorBoard |\n| Build System | CMake (C++), pyproject.toml (Python) |\n\n---\n\n## 11. Source Code\n\nGame engine source: https://github.com/electronicarts/CnC_Generals_Zero_Hour\n\nKey engine constants:\n- `LOGICFRAMES_PER_SECOND = 30` (33ms per tick)\n- Decision rate: every 10 ticks = 3 decisions/sec = 333ms\n- Engine split: GameEngine (logic) / GameEngineDevice (rendering)\n"
}
```

## C&C Generals Zero Hour Source Code Structure Analysis

### 1. DIRECTORY TREE (GeneralsMD/Code - 2-3 levels deep)

```
GeneralsMD/Code/
├── GameEngine/
│   ├── Include/
│   │   ├── Common/
│   │   ├── GameClient/

│   └── Source/

│       ├── GameLogic/
│       ├── GameNetwork/
│       └── Precompiled/
├── GameEngineDevice/
│   ├── Include/
│   │   ├── MilesAudioDevice/
│   │   ├── VideoDevice/
│   │   ├── W3DDevice/

│       ├── MilesAudioDevice/

├── Libraries/
│   ├── Include/
│   │   ├── Granny/

│   │   └── rts/
│   └── Source/
│       ├── Benchmark/
│       ├── Compression/
│       ├── DX90SDK/
│       ├── EABrowserDispatch/
│       ├── GameSpy/
│       ├── max4sdk/
│       ├── profile/
│       ├── STLport-4.5.3/

├── Main/
├── Tools/
│   ├── Babylon/
│   ├── DebugWindow/
│   ├── GUIEdit/
│   ├── ImagePacker/
│   ├── Launcher/
│   ├── MapCacheBuilder/
│   ├── ParticleEditor/
│   ├── WorldBuilder/
│   └── [other tools]
└── RTS.dsp / RTS.dsw
```

### 2. KEY TYPES AND CONSTANTS FROM GameCommon.h

**Core Timing Constants:**
- `LOGICFRAMES_PER_SECOND = 30` - Game runs at 30 logic frames per second
- `MSEC_PER_SECOND = 1000`
- Conversion helpers: `ConvertDurationFromMsecsToFrames()`, `ConvertVelocityInSecsToFrames()`, `ConvertAccelerationInSecsToFrames()`, `ConvertAngularVelocityInDegreesPerSecToRadsPerFrame()`

**Player Management:**
- `MAX_PLAYER_COUNT = 16` - Maximum simultaneous players
- `PlayerMaskType` - Bitmask to uniquely represent each player (uses UnsignedShort)
- `PLAYERMASK_ALL = 0xffff`, `PLAYERMASK_NONE = 0x0`

**General/Faction System:**
- `MAX_GLOBAL_GENERAL_TYPES = 9` - Number of playable generals (not including boss)
- `GLOBAL_GENERAL_BEGIN = 5`, `GLOBAL_GENERAL_END` range

**Difficulty Enum:**
```cpp
enum GameDifficulty {
    DIFFICULTY_EASY,
    DIFFICULTY_NORMAL,
    DIFFICULTY_HARD,
    DIFFICULTY_COUNT
};
```

**Player Type:**
```cpp
enum PlayerType {
    PLAYER_HUMAN,        // Player-controlled
    PLAYER_COMPUTER,     // AI-controlled
    PLAYERTYPE_COUNT
};
```

**Shroud Status (Fog of War):**
```cpp

  - `ApplicationHWnd` - Application window handle
  - `TheWin32Mouse` - Mouse input handler
  - Game engine via includes to `Common/GameEngine.h`, `GameClient/GameClient.h`, `GameLogic/GameLogic.h`

- Uses resource file: `RTS.RC`
- Default resolution: 800x600
- Creates single-instance mutex with GUID: `685EAFF2-3216-4265-B047-251C5F4B82F3`
- String file: `data\Generals.str`, Localized: `data\%s\Generals.csf`
- Load screen bitmap support (`gLoadScreenBitmap`)
- Debug logging with optional prefix (`gAppPrefix`)

### 7. KEY GAME STATE STRUCTURES

**Main Game State:**
- `/GameEngine/Include/GameLogic/GameLogic.h` - Core game state singleton
  - Manages all objects, players, teams, scoring
  - Frame counter and timing
  - Game modes (GAME_SINGLE_PLAYER, GAME_LAN, GAME_SKIRMISH, GAME_REPLAY, GAME_SHELL, GAME_INTERNET, GAME_NONE)
  - CRC validation for multiplayer
  - Load/save functionality

**Player Structure:**
- `/GameEngine/Include/Common/Player.h` - Player entity
  - Persistent data (rank, academy stats) and transient mission data
  - `PlayerRelationMap` - Relationship map between players
  - `SpecialPowerReadyTimerType` - Special ability cooldown tracking
  - Supports human and computer-controlled players
  - Team affiliations, science/upgrade tracking, money, energy
  - `NUM_HOTKEY_SQUADS = 10` - Hotkey group management

**Object (Unit/Building) Structure:**
- `/GameEngine/Include/GameLogic/Object.h` - Base game object
  - Inherits from: `Thing` and `Snapshot`
  - Unique `ObjectID` for referencing
  - Owner `Player` reference
  - Team membership via `AIGroup`
  - Physical properties (position, orientation, velocity)
  - Drawable association for rendering
  - Status bits (shrouded, experience, health)
  - Module system:
    - `BehaviorModuleInterface` - Behavioral updates
    - `BodyModuleInterface` - Physical body
    - `DamageModuleInterface` - Damage tracking
    - `UpdateModuleInterface` - Unit-specific updates
    - `WeaponSetFlags`, `WeaponStatus` - Weapon management
  - Supports: experience/veterancy, special powers, upgrades, contain modules
  - Polygon trigger tracking for area effects

**Object Types:**
- `/GameEngine/Include/GameLogic/ObjectTypes.h` - Unit/building type definitions

**Related Structures:**
- `/GameEngine/Include/Common/Team.h` - Team grouping
- `/GameEngine/Include/GameLogic/Module/StealthUpdate.h` - Stealth/invisibility
- `/GameEngine/Include/GameLogic/WeaponSet.h` - Weapon configurations
- `/GameEngine/Include/GameLogic/Damage.h` - Damage calculation
- `/GameEngine/Include/Common/Upgrade.h` - Upgrade tracking
- `/GameEngine/Include/GameLogic/ObjectCreationList.h` - Object iteration
- `/GameEngine/Include/GameLogic/ObjectScriptStatusBits.h` - Script-driven status

├── Behavior/ - Behavioral modules
├── Destroy/ - Destruction logic
├── Contain/ - Container modules
├── Upgrade/ - Upgrade application
├── Update/ - Update modules
├── Die/ - Death logic
├── Body/ - Physical body
├── Collide/ - Collision
├── SpecialPower/ - Ability system
├── Create/ - Creation logic
└── Helper/ - Helper classes
```

**Important Memory Management:**
- Uses memory pools: `MEMORY_POOL_GLUE_WITH_USERLOOKUP_CREATE` macro
- Snapshot pattern for save/load: objects inherit from `Snapshot` class
- XFer system for serialization (binary file format)

This is a sophisticated, modular RTS engine with 30 FPS logic updates, advanced AI with state machines and pathfinding, module-based object architecture, and complete multiplayer/networking support.

game_state.py**\n\n```python\n# src/zh_copilot/state/game_state.py\n\"\"\"Unified game state representation used by both training and deployment.\"\"\"\n\nfrom __future__ import annotations\n\nfrom dataclasses import dataclass, field\nfrom typing import Optional\n\nimport numpy as np\n\nfrom zh_copilot.config import STATE, ENGINE\n\n\n# --- Action types ---\nACTION_NAMES = [\n    \"build_structure\",\n    \"train_unit\",\n    \"sell_building\",\n    \"set_rally_point\",\n    \"move\",\n    \"attack_move\",\n    \"patrol\",\n    \"guard\",\n    \"enter_garrison\",\n    \"attack_target\",\n    \"force_fire\",\n    \"retreat\",\n    \"use_ability\",\n    \"research_upgrade\",\n    \"choose_general_power\",\n    \"activate_power\",\n    \"select_group\",\n    \"set_waypoint\",\n    \"garrison_building\",\n    \"exit_garrison\",\n    \"noop\",\n]\nNOOP_ACTION_TYPE = ACTION_NAMES.index(\"noop\")\n\nSTRATEGY_BUTTONS = [\"attack\", \"defend\", \"rush\", \"farm\", \"turtle\", \"auto\"]\nAUTO_STRATEGY_IDX = STRATEGY_BUTTONS.index(\"auto\")\n\n\n@dataclass\nclass SpatialMap:\n    \"\"\"Spatial grid representation of the game map.\n\n    Shape: (channels, resolution, resolution).\n    Channels encode per-player unit density, DPS, HP, buildings, terrain, fog.\n    \"\"\"\n\n    data: np.ndarray  # (C, H, W) float32\n\n    @staticmethod\n    def empty(\n        resolution: int = STATE.spatial_resolution,\n        channels: int = STATE.spatial_channels,\n    ) -> SpatialMap:\n        return SpatialMap(data=np.zeros((channels, resolution, resolution), dtype=np.float32))\n\n\n@dataclass\nclass Entity:\n    \"\"\"Batch of entity feature vectors with attention mask.\n\n    features: (max_entities, feature_dim) — per-entity attributes.\n    mask: (max_entities,) — True if entity slot is occupied.\n    \"\"\"\n\n    features: np.ndarray  # (N, D) float32\n    mask: np.ndarray  # (N,) bool\n\n    @staticmethod\n    def empty_batch(\n        n: int = STATE.max_entities,\n        feature_dim: int = STATE.entity_feature_dim,\n    ) -> Entity:\n        return Entity(\n            features=np.zeros((n, feature_dim), dtype=np.float32),\n            mask=np.zeros(n, dtype=bool),\n        )\n\n    @property\n    def count(self) -> int:\n        return int(self.mask.sum())\n\n\n@dataclass\nclass PlayerState:\n    \"\"\"Per-player economic and faction state for all player slots.\"\"\"\n\n    money: list[float]\n    power_surplus: list[float]\n    supply_used: list[float]\n    supply_cap: list[float]\n    num_units: list[int]\n    tech_level: list[int]\n    faction_id: list[int]\n    team_id: list[int]\n\n    @staticmethod\n    def empty(max_players: int = ENGINE.max_players) -> PlayerState:\n        return PlayerState(\n            money=[0.0] * max_players,\n            power_surplus=[0.0] * max_players,\n            supply_used=[0.0] * max_players,\n            supply_cap=[0.0] * max_players,\n            num_units=[0] * max_players,\n            tech_level=[0] * max_players,\n            faction_id=[0] * max_players,\n            team_id=[0] * max_players,\n        )\n\n\n@dataclass\nclass GlobalState:\n    \"\"\"Game-level scalar state.\"\"\"\n\n    game_time: float = 0.0\n    game_mode: str = \"1v1\"\n    map_id: int = 0\n    human_strategy_override: int = AUTO_STRATEGY_IDX\n\n    @staticmethod\n    def empty() -> GlobalState:\n        return GlobalState()\n\n\n@dataclass\nclass GameState:\n    \"\"\"Complete game observation at a single decision step.\n\n    This is the unified format consumed by the AI model.\n    Both the headless engine (training) and screen capture (deploy) produce this.\n    \"\"\"\n\n    spatial: SpatialMap\n    entities: Entity\n    players: PlayerState\n    global_state: GlobalState\n\n    @staticmethod\n    def empty() -> GameState:\n        return GameState(\n            spatial=SpatialMap.empty(),\n            entities=Entity.empty_batch(),\n            players=PlayerState.empty(),\n            global_state=GlobalState.empty(),\n        )\n\n\n@dataclass\nclass Action:\n    \"\"\"Single action output from the AI model.\"\"\"\n\n    action_type: int\n    target_entity_idx: Optional[int] = None\n    target_position: Optional[tuple[float, float]] = None\n    modifier: int = 0\n\n    @property\n    def is_noop(self) -> bool:\n        return self.action_type == NOOP_ACTION_TYPE\n\n    @staticmethod\n    def noop() -> Action:\n        return Action(action_type=NOOP_ACTION_TYPE)\n```\n\n- [ ] **Step 5: Run tests to verify they used by both training and deployment.\"\"\"\n\nfrom __future__ import annotations\n\nfrom dataclasses import dataclass\nfrom typing import Optional\n\nimport numpy as np\n\nfrom zh_copilot.config import STATE, ENGINE\n\n\n# --- Action types ---\nACTION_NAMES = [\n    \"build_structure\",\n    \"train_unit\",\n    \"sell_building\",\n    \"set_rally_point\",\n    \"move\",\n ACTION_NAMES.index(\"noop\")\n\nSTRATEGY_BUTTONS = [\"attack\", \"defend\", \"rush\", \"farm\", \"turtle\", \"auto\"]\nAUTO_STRATEGY_IDX = STRATEGY_BUTTONS.index(\"auto\")\n\n\n@dataclass\nclass SpatialMap:\n    \"\"\"Spatial grid representation of the game map.\n\n    Shape: (channels, resolution, resolution).\n    Channels encode per-player unit density, DPS, HP, buildings, terrain, fog.\n    \"\"\"\n\n    data: np.ndarray  # (C, H, W) float32\n\n    @staticmethod\n    def empty(\n        resolution: int = STATE.spatial_resolution,\n        channels: int = STATE.spatial_channels,\n    ) -> SpatialMap:\n        return SpatialMap(data=np.zeros((channels, resolution, resolution), dtype=np.float32))\n\n\n@dataclass\nclass Entity:\n    \"\"\"Batch of entity feature vectors with attention mask.\n\n    features: (max_entities, feature_dim) — per-entity attributes.\n    mask: (max_entities,) — True if entity slot is occupied.\n    \"\"\"\n\n    features: np.ndarray  # (N, D) float32\n    mask: np.ndarray  # (N,) bool\n\n    @staticmethod\n    def empty_batch(\n        n: int = STATE.max_entities,\n        feature_dim: int = STATE.entity_feature_dim,\n    ) -> Entity:\n        return Entity(\n            features=np.zeros((n, feature_dim), dtype=np.float32),\n            mask=np.zeros(n, dtype=bool),\n        )\n\n    @property\n    def count(self) -> int:\n        return int(self.mask.sum())\n\n\n@dataclass\nclass PlayerState:\n    \"\"\"Per-player economic and faction state for all player slots.\"\"\"\n\n    money: list[float]\n    power_surplus: list[float]\n    supply_used: list[float]\n    supply_cap: list[float]\n    num_units: list[int]\n    tech_level: list[int]\n    faction_id: list[int]\n    team_id: list[int]\n\n    @staticmethod\n    def empty(max_players: int = ENGINE.max_players) -> PlayerState:\n        return PlayerState(\n            money=[0.0] * max_players,\n            power_surplus=[0.0] * max_players,\n            supply_used=[0.0] * max_players,\n            supply_cap=[0.0] * max_players,\n            num_units=[0] * max_players,\n            tech_level=[0] * max_players,\n            faction_id=[0] * max_players,\n            team_id=[0] * max_players,\n        )\n\n\n@dataclass\nclass GlobalState:\n    \"\"\"Game-level scalar state.\"\"\"\n\n    game_time: float = 0.0\n    game_mode: str = \"1v1\"\n    map_id: int = 0\n    human_strategy_override: int = AUTO_STRATEGY_IDX\n\n    @staticmethod\n    def empty() -> GlobalState:\n        return GlobalState()\n\n\n@dataclass\nclass GameState:\n    \"\"\"Complete game observation at a single decision step.\n\n    This is the unified format consumed by the AI model.\n    Both the headless engine (training) and screen capture (deploy) produce this.\n    \"\"\"\n\n    spatial: SpatialMap\n    entities: Entity\n    players: PlayerState\n    global_state: GlobalState\n\n    @staticmethod\n    def empty() -> GameState:\n        return GameState(\n            spatial=SpatialMap.empty(),\n            entities=Entity.empty_batch(),\n            players=PlayerState.empty(),\n            global_state=GlobalState.empty(),\n        )\n\n\n@dataclass\nclass Action:\n    \"\"\"Single action output from the AI model.\"\"\"\n\n    action_type: int\n    target_entity_idx: Optional[int] = None\n    target_position: Optional[tuple[float, float]] = None\n    modifier: int = 0\n\n    @property\n    def is_noop(self) -> bool:\n        return self.action_type == NOOP_ACTION_TYPE\n\n    @staticmethod\n    def noop() -> Action:\n        return Action(action_type=NOOP_ACTION_TYPE)\n```\n\n**Step 3: Create tests/test_game_state.py**\n\n```python\n\"\"\"Tests for game state representation.\"\"\"\n\nimport numpy as np\n\nfrom zh_copilot.state.game_state import (\n    Action,\n    Entity,\n    GameState,\n    GlobalState,\n    PlayerState,\n    SpatialMap,\n)\n\n\ndef test_spatial_map_shape():\n    sm = SpatialMap.empty(resolution=128, channels=40)\n    assert sm.data.shape == (40, 128, 128)\n    assert sm.data.dtype == np.float32\n\n\ndef test_entity_array_shape():\n    entities = Entity.empty_batch(n=1024, feature_dim=32)\n    assert entities.features.shape == (1024, 32)\n    assert entities.mask.shape == (1024,)\n    assert entities.mask.sum() == 0  # all masked out (no entities)\n\n\ndef test_player_state_slots():\n    ps = PlayerState.empty(max_players=8)\n    assert len(ps.money) == 8\n    assert len(ps.faction_id) == 8\n    assert all(m == 0 for m in ps.money)\n\n\ndef test_global_state_contains_game_mode():\n    gs = GlobalState.empty()\n    assert gs.game_time == 0.0\n    assert gs.game_mode == \"1v1\"\n    assert gs.human_strategy_override == 5 None\n    assert state.global_state is not None\n\n\ndef test_action_creation():\n    action = Action(\n        action_type=0,\n        target_entity_idx=None,\n        target_position=None,\n        modifier=0,\n    )\n    assert action.action_type == 0\n    assert action.is_noop is False\n\n\ndef test_noop_action():\n    action = Action.noop()\n    assert action.is_noop is True\n    assert action.action_type == 20  # no-op is last action type\n```\n\n**Step 4: Run the tests**\n\nFirst make sure you have numpy available. Based on the Claude Code documentation, here are the exact settings and options to auto-approve tool permissions:

## Auto-Approval Options

There are several approaches depending on your use case:

### 1. **Auto Mode** (Recommended for most users)
Auto mode eliminates permission prompts while maintaining background safety checks via a classifier that verifies actions match your intent.

**At startup:**
```bash
claude --enable-auto-mode --permission-mode auto
```

**In settings.json:**
```json
{
  "permissions": {
    "defaultMode": "auto"
  }
}
```

**Requirements:**
- Team, Enterprise, or API plan
- Claude Sonnet 4.6 or Claude Opus 4.6
- Not available on Haiku or claude-3 models

**Cost:** Higher token usage due to classifier calls that evaluate each action

### 2. **Bypass Permissions Mode** (Only for isolated environments)
Completely disables permission checks with no safety safeguards. Only use in containers or VMs without network access.

**At startup:**
```bash
claude --permission-mode bypassPermissions
# or
claude --dangerously-skip-permissions
```

**In settings.json:**
```json
{
  "permissions": {
    "defaultMode": "bypassPermissions"
  }
}
```

**Restrictions:** Still prompts for writes to `.git`, `.vscode`, `.idea`, and `.claude` (except for `.claude/commands`, `.claude/agents`, `.claude/skills`)

### 3. **Accept Edits Mode** (For manual review, not full auto-approval)
Auto-approves file edits but still prompts for shell commands and network requests.

```bash
claude --permission-mode acceptEdits
```

### 4. **Permission Rules + Hooks** (For granular control)
Pre-approve specific tools via allow rules in settings.json:

```json
{
  "permissions": {
    "allow": [
      "Bash(*)",
      "WebFetch",
      "Edit(*)"
    ]
  }
}
```

Or use a `PermissionRequest` hook to auto-answer prompts based on custom logic.

## Your Current Setup

Your settings already have `skipAutoPermissionPrompt: true`, which skips the confirmation dialog but still requires tool execution to match allow rules. For true auto-approval of all tools without any prompting, use **`--permission-mode bypassPermissions`** in isolated environments or **auto mode** in regular development.

**Option A — Auto mode** (safe, has guardrails):

claude --permission-mode auto

**Option B — Bypass all** (no prompts at all, use in safe environments only):
```bash
claude --dangerously-skip-permissions
```

For our project, Option B is fine — we're working locally on your own repo. Restart the session with:

```

Or to make it permanent for this project, add to your settings:

  "permissions": {
    "defaultMode": "bypassPermissions"
  }
}
